The proof that ρ^z is cash-additive uses only the definition ρ^z(X) = inf{ξ : β(X+ξ) ≥ z}; the proof that ρ^z is positively homogeneous uses only scale invariance of β; the proof that z ↦ ρ^z(X) is monotone and continuous uses only (3b) + (4). Monotonicity in X of β enters ONLY in proving monotonicity of ρ^z. For the loss ratio, Assa's 'translation to break-even' axiom (c ↦ LR(X+c) strictly monotone towards 1) is exactly axiom (3b) on each branch {LR<1}, {LR>1}, so the induced family ρ^β(X) := inf{m : LR(X+m) ≤ β} (β<1) is well defined, cash-additive and positively homogeneous WITHOUT any monotonicity of LR in X, and uniqueness of the family holds verbatim. Prop. 1 of Assa is the specialization of BBN Prop. 3.3 to the canonical family.
  [3] Biagini, S. & Pınar, M. Ç. (2013), 'The best gain-loss ratio is a poor performance measure', SIAM J. Financial Math. 4(1), 228-242, arXiv:1209.6439: Lemma 3.1, Theorem 3.3, Corollary 3.5. (confidence: high (read online, arXiv:1209.6439))
      STATEMENT: Setting: S an ℝ^d-valued semimartingale on [0,T] with S*_T = sup_{t≤T}|S_t| ∈ L^1(P) (Assumption 2.1); K = set of terminal gains of admissible strategies; α(X) := E[X^+]/E[X^-] (Bernardo–Ledoit gain-loss ratio, defined when X ≠ 0 and X^+ or X^- integrable); α*(B) := sup{ α(B+K) : K ∈ K, B+K ≠ 0 } for B ∈ L^1. LEMMA 3.1: α*: L^1 → [α*(0),+∞] is non-decreasing (B_1 ≤ B_2 ⇒ α*(B_1) ≤ α*(B_2)), quasi-concave (α*(cB_1+(1−c)B_2) ≥ min(α*(B_1),α*(B_2))), scale invariant (α*(cB)=α*(B), c>0) and continuous from below (B_n ↑ B ⇒ α*(B_n) ↑ α*(B)); hence an acceptability index in the sense of Biagini–Bion-Nadal. THEOREM 3.3: for B ∈ L^1 the following are equivalent: (i) α*(B) < +∞; (ii) E_Q[B] ≤ 0 for some Q ∈ M_∞ := { equivalent martingale measures Q with density Z = dQ/dP bounded and bounded away from 0 }. If either holds, α*(B) = min_{ Q ∈ M_∞, E_Q[B] ≤ 0 } ess sup Z / ess inf Z, and when +∞ > α*(B) > α*(0) the constraint can be taken as E_Q[B] = 0. COROLLARY 3.5: α*(B) = sup{ λ ≥ 1 : E_Q[B] > 0 ∀ Q ∈ Q_λ ∩ M } with Q_λ := {Q ≪ P : ∃y>0, E[V_λ(y dQ/dP)] < ∞} (sup ∅ = α*(0)). The paper shows that in most continuous-time models α*(B) is +∞ or not attained, and that scale invariance produces undesirable behaviour.
      USE: Two uses. (a) The dual object of the (monotone) gain-loss acceptability index is the NON-monotone ratio Z ↦ ess sup Z / ess inf Z of the pricing kernel, which is itself a 'loss ratio' of Choquet type: ess sup Z = C_h(Z) with h = 1_{(0,1]}, ess inf Z = C_g(Z) with g = 1_{{1}}. So a ratio of two increasing functionals (exactly the F2 structure) is the natural dual of an acceptability index: LR_{(h,g)}(Z) ≤ λ ⇔ Z/E[Z] ∈ D_{λ−1} (CM's representing set for GLR). This suggests characterizing LR = C_h/C_g not as an index of X but through the cones it generates on the dual side: {X : LR(X) ≤ β} = {X : max_{Q∈core(h)} E_Q X ≤ β min_{Q'∈anticore(g)} E_{Q'} X} ('ask ≤ β·bid'). (b) The warning: a scale-invariant ratio with unbounded range on infinite spaces may be +∞ or non-attained; for the loss ratio this is harmless because 0 ≤ X bounded gives C_g(X) > 0 whenever X ≠ 0 and g>0 on (0,1].
  [4] Bernardo, A. E. & Ledoit, O. (2000), 'Gain, loss, and asset pricing', J. Political Economy 108(1), 144-172, Theorem 1 (duality) — exact statement from memory; read in the form of Pınar, M. Ç. (2010), 'Gain-loss pricing under ambiguity of measure', ESAIM: COCV 16(1), 132-147 (Bilkent repository copy), Lemma 4.1, Theorem 4.2 and Corollary 4.6. (confidence: high for Pınar's statements (read online); medium for the verbatim Bernardo–Ledoit Theorem 1 (JPE page not accessible))
      STATEMENT: Fix λ > 1, a reference probability P* and a market with prices Z_0 ∈ ℝ^{J+1}_+ at 0 and payoffs Z_T ∈ L^1_+(P*). A portfolio Θ is a λ gain-loss opportunity if Z_0·Θ ≤ 0 and E*[(Z_T·Θ)^+] − λ E*[(Z_T·Θ)^-] > 0. LEMMA 4.1: for Z ∈ L^1 and λ > 1, λE[Z^-] − E[Z^+] = sup{ −E[ηZ] : η ∈ L^∞, 1 ≤ η ≤ λ a.s. }. COROLLARY 4.6 (Bernardo–Ledoit case): the following are equivalent: (a) the primal problem max E*[(Z_T·Θ)^+] − λE*[(Z_T·Θ)^-] s.t. Z_0·Θ ≤ 0 is bounded; (b) its dual is feasible; (c) there is no λ gain-loss opportunity; (d) there exists an equivalent martingale measure Q with dQ/dP* ∈ [V, Vλ] P*-a.s. for some constant V > 0, i.e. ess sup(dQ/dP*)/ess inf(dQ/dP*) ≤ λ. THEOREM 4.2 extends this to an ambiguity set P of priors: no robust λ gain-loss opportunity ⇔ ∃ P ∈ P and an EMM Q with dQ/dP ∈ [V,Vλ]. (Bernardo–Ledoit's original Theorem 1 is the finite-state version: sup over portfolios of E*[X^+]/E*[X^-] ≤ L iff some pricing kernel m with ess sup(m/m*)/ess inf(m/m*) ≤ L, where m* is the benchmark kernel.)
      USE: Shows the gain-loss bound is dual to a bound on the LOSS RATIO (sup/inf ratio) of the density. The identity in Lemma 4.1 is the prototype of Assa's association: 'GLR(Z) ≥ λ' ⇔ the SIGNED linear-in-η functional sup_{1≤η≤λ}(−E[ηZ]) ≤ 0 — a Choquet-type functional with a non-monotone 'distortion' (−1 on gains, +λ on losses), structurally the same as (h − βg)/(1−β) in F7. It also shows that an iff theorem of the form 'ratio ≤ λ for all positions ⇔ existence of a kernel with a loss-ratio bound' is available by Lagrangian duality on L^1 without any monotonicity of the ratio.
  [5] Bellini, F., Klar, B. & Müller, A. (2018), 'Expectiles, Omega ratios and stochastic ordering', Methodol. Comput. Appl. Probab. 20(3), 855-873 (KIT open repository copy): Eq. (3)-(6), Theorem 2, Definition 6, Theorem 8, Corollary 13. (confidence: high (read online))
      STATEMENT: For X ∈ L^1, α ∈ (0,1), the expectile e_X(α) is the unique minimizer of E[ℓ_α(X−t) − ℓ_α(X)], ℓ_α(x) = αx² (x≥0), (1−α)x² (x<0), characterized by the first-order condition α E[(X−e)^+] = (1−α) E[(X−e)^-] (Eq. 3). Omega ratio: Ω_X(t) = E[(X−t)^+]/E[(X−t)^-] (Eq. 4), for t with positive denominator. THEOREM 2: let X ∈ L^1, m = ess inf X, M = ess sup X; then Ω_X: (m,M) → (0,+∞) is strictly positive, continuous and strictly decreasing, with lim_{t↓m} Ω_X(t) = +∞, lim_{t↑M} Ω_X(t) = 0 and Ω_X(E X) = 1. Eq. (5)-(6): Ω_X(e_X(α)) = (1−α)/α, i.e. e_X(α) = Ω_X^{-1}((1−α)/α): the expectile is the inverse function of the Omega ratio ('one-to-one relation between expectiles and Omega ratios'). THEOREM 8: for X,Y ∈ L^1, m = max(ess inf X, ess inf Y), M = min(ess sup X, ess sup Y), TFAE: (a) X ≤_e Y (e_X(α) ≤ e_Y(α) ∀α∈(0,1)); (b) Ω_X(x) ≤ Ω_Y(x) ∀x ∈ (m,M); (c) π_X(x)(x−E Y) ≤ π_Y(x)(x−E X) ∀x ∈ (m,M), π the stop-loss transform. The expectile order is neither implied by nor implies ≤_icx (Example 15) and Ω_X ≤ Ω_Y on ℝ does NOT imply X ≤_st Y (correcting Remillard 2013).
      USE: The Omega ratio at threshold t is a ratio of two functionals of X−t and is the exact prototype of 'translation to break-even' (F1): t ↦ Ω_X(t) is strictly monotone and the level Ω = (1−α)/α is attained exactly at the expectile — i.e. an ASSOCIATION 'Ω_X(t) = β ⇔ V_β(X−t) = 0' with the identification function V_β(Y) = E[Y^+] − β E[Y^-] = ∫ Y d(signed distortion) that is cash-affine but NOT monotone in the pair. This is the elicitability/identification reading (F6) already present in the literature: the loss ratio should be viewed, like Omega, as the inverse of a one-parameter family of identification functionals, and the natural iff axiom on LR is strict monotonicity + continuity of c ↦ LR(X+c) (Theorem 2-type) rather than monotonicity in X. Theorem 8 also shows what kind of ORDER a ratio can be consistent with: not ≤_st (hence F2 is the generic situation for ratios), but a bespoke order generated by the family {ρ_β}.
  [6] Righi, M. B. (2024), 'Star-shaped acceptability indexes', Insurance: Mathematics and Economics 117, 170-181, arXiv:2110.08630: Definition 2.1, Theorem 3.3, Corollary 3.8, Definition 5.1, Proposition 5.2, Section 6 (gain-loss) and Section 7 (reward-deviation). (confidence: high (read online, arXiv:2110.08630))
      STATEMENT: α: L^∞ → [0,∞]. Axioms: (i) monotonicity; (ii) quasi-concavity; (iii) scale invariance; (iv) star-shapedness: α(0)=∞ and α(λX) ≤ α(X) ∀λ ≥ 1 (⇔ λ ↦ α(λX) non-increasing on [0,∞), Prop. 3.1); (v) Fatou continuity: X_n → X a.s., sup_n‖X_n‖_∞ < ∞, α(X_n) ≥ x ∀n ⇒ α(X) ≥ x; (vi) weak expectation consistency. A star-shaped acceptability index satisfies (i),(iv),(v),(vi). THEOREM 3.3 (sup ∅ = 0): TFAE (i) α is a star-shaped acceptability index; (ii) there is a decreasing family {A_x}_{x>0} of star-shaped acceptance sets, each closed under bounded a.s. convergence, with α(X) = sup{x>0 : X ∈ A_x}; (iii) there is an increasing family {ρ_x}_{x>0} of Fatou-continuous normalized (ρ_x(0)=0) star-shaped monetary risk measures with α(X) = sup{x>0 : ρ_x(X) ≤ 0}; each implies (iv) α = max of a family of quasi-concave acceptability indices. COROLLARY 3.8: equivalently α(X) = sup{x>0 : min_{ρ∈R_x} ρ(X) ≤ 0} for increasing families R_x of Fatou-continuous convex risk measures, or via increasing families of convex weak-lsc penalty functions. PROPOSITION 5.2 (RAROC): for RAROC(X) = μ(X)/ρ(X) (μ a reward functional, ρ a risk measure, with conventions when ρ(X) ≤ 0), the associated family is ρ_x(X) = (−μ(X) + x ρ(X))/(1+x) with acceptance sets A_x = {μ(X)/ρ(X) ≥ x}; RAROC is a coherent (quasi-concave) acceptability index if ρ is coherent (convex) — stated as sufficient only. GLR (Section 6): E[X]/E[X^-] is coherent with representing family ρ_x = EVaR^{1/(2+x)} (expectile-VaR); the variant E[X^+]/E[X^-] is 'not quasi-concave due to convexity of the numerator' but IS star-shaped, with ρ_x = EVaR^{1/(1+x)}. Sharpe-type ratios 'lack monotonicity' and are treated only through a monotone reward-deviation surrogate (Def. 7.2, Prop. 7.3).

(2) Theorem 3.3 shows the CM machinery survives dropping quasi-concavity (replacing convex cones by star-shaped sets); by the same token it should survive dropping monotonicity, replacing 'monetary risk measure' by 'cash-additive, positively homogeneous, Fatou-continuous functional' (the level-set/sup correspondence in the proof of Thm 3.3 (i)⇔(ii)⇔(iii) does not use monotonicity except to transfer it). (3) The GLR example E[X^+]/E[X^-] being star-shaped but not quasi-concave is a warning for F4: a Choquet loss ratio is generally only comonotone-quasi-concave, not quasi-concave.

      STATEMENT: X a locally convex topological vector space with a preorder given by a convex cone K; K° its polar. A risk measure is ρ: X → [−∞,+∞] quasi-convex (ρ(λx+(1−λ)y) ≤ max(ρ(x),ρ(y))) and monotone (x ≤ y ⇒ ρ(x) ≥ ρ(y)). THEOREM 1.10 / PROP 1.16: ρ ↦ (A_m)_{m∈ℝ}, A_m := {x : ρ(x) ≤ m}, is a one-to-one correspondence between risk measures and 'risk acceptance families' (A_m) (each A_m convex and monotone, A_m ⊂ A_n for m ≤ n, right-continuous A_m = ∩_{n>m} A_n), with inverse ρ_A(x) = inf{m : x ∈ A_m}. Moreover: ρ convex ⇔ A_{λm+(1−λ)n} ⊃ λA_m + (1−λ)A_n; ρ positively homogeneous ⇔ λA_m = A_{λm} ∀λ>0; ρ SCALE INVARIANT (ρ(λx)=ρ(x)) ⇔ λA_m = A_m ∀λ>0 (acceptance sets are cones). THEOREM 2.7: every lower semicontinuous risk measure ρ has a unique robust representation ρ(x) = sup_{x*∈K°} R(x*, ⟨x*,−x⟩) where R: K°×ℝ → [−∞,∞] is the maximal risk function (jointly concave in a suitable sense, non-decreasing and left-continuous in the second argument, with right-continuous version R⁺ upper semicontinuous in the first argument), R built from the minimal penalty α_min(x*,m) = sup_{x∈A_m}⟨x*,−x⟩ by left inversion; THEOREM 2.8: for X = L^∞ the sup can be restricted to probability densities (positive, integrable, expectation one). (Cerreia-Vioglio, Maccheroni, Marinacci & Montrucchio (2011), 'Risk measures: rationality and diversification', Math. Finance 21(4), prove the same for monotone quasi-convex lsc maps on M-spaces: ρ(X) = sup_Q R(E_Q[X], Q); Peri (2012) Thm 33-36 shows the two representations coincide.)

For the loss ratio: LR scale invariant ⇔ all sublevel sets {LR ≤ β} are cones; the canonical family is positively homogeneous ⇔ the sets {ρ_β ≤ m} scale as λA_m = A_{λm}. The robust representation (Thm 2.7) does need monotonicity (K° = positive functionals), which is exactly where LR fails; the substitute for the dual side is the signed-Choquet / bounded-variation representation (F7, Wang–Wei–Willmot) rather than probability measures. So DK tells us precisely which half of the theory is salvageable: primal level-set structure yes, probabilistic dual no.

      STATEMENT: X = Σ_i X_i, ρ a risk measure with ρ(X) = f_ρ(1,…,1) where f_ρ(u) := ρ(Σ u_i X_i). RORAC(X) := E[X]/ρ(X); portfolio-related RORAC of asset i: RORAC(X_i|X) := E[X_i]/ρ(X_i|X). DEFINITION 2.2: contributions ρ(X_i|X) satisfy full allocation if Σ_i ρ(X_i|X) = ρ(X); they are RORAC compatible if there are ε_i > 0 with RORAC(X_i|X) > RORAC(X) ⇒ RORAC(X + hX_i) > RORAC(X) for all 0 < h < ε_i. PROPOSITION 2.1 (= Tasche 1999 Thm 4.4): if f_ρ is continuously differentiable and there are RORAC-compatible contributions for ARBITRARY expected values μ_1,…,μ_n of X_1,…,X_n, then ρ(X_i|X) is uniquely determined as ρ_Euler(X_i|X) = d/dh ρ(X+hX_i)|_{h=0} = ∂f_ρ/∂u_i(1,…,1). Conversely Euler contributions are always RORAC compatible. Section 2.2: for ρ with continuously differentiable f_ρ, full allocation and RORAC compatibility can hold simultaneously IF AND ONLY IF ρ is positively homogeneous of degree 1 (Euler's theorem, Thm A.1: f ∈ C^1 on open U ⊂ ℝ^n is homogeneous of degree τ ⇔ τ f(u) = Σ u_i ∂f/∂u_i(u)). (Stoughton & Zechner (2007), 'Optimal capital allocation using RAROC and EVA', J. Financial Intermediation 16, 312-342, give an equilibrium rather than an axiomatic characterization and contain no iff theorem of the present type.)

The loss-ratio analogue is immediate and does not need monotonicity: d/dh LR(X+hY)|_{h=0} = (ρ'(X;Y) − LR(X) Π'(X;Y))/Π(X) = (1−β) ρ_β'(X;Y)/Π(X) with β = LR(X), i.e. the Gateaux derivative of the ASSOCIATED signed functional ρ_β at the current level β. Hence 'LR-compatible' loss-ratio contributions (adding business line Y lowers LR iff its contribution ratio is below the portfolio LR) exist iff they are Euler contributions of C_h and C_g (both positively homogeneous), which is a usable 'axiom on LR alone' (local improvement principle) equivalent to positive homogeneity of the pair.

      STATEMENT: X ⊂ L^p(Ω,F,P) convex set of returns. A reward-risk ratio is α(X) = θ(X)^+/ρ(X)^+ with reward measure θ: X → ℝ∪{±∞} and risk measure ρ: X → ℝ∪{+∞}. Axioms: (M) monotonicity: X ≤ Y a.s. ⇒ α(X) ≤ α(Y); (Q) quasi-concavity: α(δX+(1−δ)Y) ≥ min(α(X),α(Y)), δ ∈ [0,1]; (S) scale invariance: α(δX) = α(X) for δ>0 with δX ∈ X; (D) distribution-based: α(X) depends only on the law of X under P. Structural result (Cheridito–Kromer Prop. 2.x, from memory; the 'ESG' version is Torri et al. Prop. 5 with the same proof): if θ is monotone non-decreasing, concave and positively homogeneous and ρ is monotone non-increasing, convex and positively homogeneous (a coherent risk measure, or a deviation measure made monotone), then α = θ^+/ρ^+ satisfies (M),(Q),(S); if both are law-invariant also (D). Classification of classical ratios (Cheridito–Kromer Table; Torri et al. §5): Sharpe ratio: (Q),(S),(D) but not (M); Omega ratio E[(X−τ)^+]/E[(X−τ)^-]: (M),(D), (S) only if τ = 0, not (Q) (numerator convex); gain-loss E[X]/E[X^-]: all four; RAROC with coherent ρ: all four; STARR (E[X]/ES_α): all four; Sortino–Satchell: all four; Rachev ratio ES-upper/ES-lower: (M),(S),(D) not (Q); Farinelli–Tibiletti: (M),(D) only.
      USE: Confirms the pattern behind F2 and F4: a ratio numerator/denominator inherits (M) exactly when numerator is increasing and denominator is DEcreasing in X; (Q) exactly when numerator concave and denominator convex (quasi-concavity of a ratio of a concave positive function over a convex positive function). For LR = ρ/Π both are increasing, so (M) fails generically (F2 proves it fails ALWAYS unless LR ≡ 1 in the Choquet class), while (Q) needs ρ concave and Π convex ('conic picture' with Π a bid price, F4) or the reverse for quasi-CONVEXITY of LR ('actuarial picture'). No iff is proved in this strand — all results are sufficient conditions — which is why the identification route (F3) rather than the axiom-inheritance route must carry the converse.

      STATEMENT: X ∈ L^p, p ∈ [1,∞); σ_p(X) := min_{c∈ℝ} ‖X−c‖_p. Monotone Sharpe ratio: S_p(X) := sup{ E[Y]/σ_p(Y) : Y ∈ L^p, Y ≤ X a.s. }. THEOREM 1 (X ∈ L^p, E X > 0, P(X<0) > 0): for p ∈ (1,∞), 1/p+1/q = 1, (S_p(X))^q = max_{a,b∈ℝ}(…) (a two-parameter finite-dimensional max); for p = 1,2: 1/(1+(S_p(X))^p) = min_{c∈ℝ} E[(1−cX)_+^p]; dual form S_p(X) = inf{ ‖R‖_q : R ∈ L^q, R ≤ 1 a.s., E R = 0, E[RX] = 1 }. THEOREM 3: S_p is quasi-concave ({S_p ≥ c} convex), scale invariant, monotone, and consistent with second-order stochastic dominance; S_p(X) = +∞ iff X ≥ 0 a.s. (arbitrage consistency); for p>1, E X < 0 ⇒ S_p(X)=0 and E X > 0 ⇒ S_p(X)>0. The classical Sharpe ratio is not monotone (counterexample X ~ N(1,1), Y = X ∧ 1). Hence S_p satisfies the four Cherny–Madan axioms (quasi-concavity, monotonicity, scale invariance, Fatou in L^∞) and the CM representation applies to it.
      USE: Shows the standard repair of a non-monotone scale-invariant ratio: monotone hull sup_{Y ≤ X} ratio(Y), after which the CM theorem applies and the hull is an acceptability index. For the loss ratio the analogous hull inf_{Y ≥ X} LR(Y) or sup_{Y ≤ X} LR(Y) is degenerate (one can push LR(Y) to any value by adding a layer, cf. the nested-layer argument in F2), so monotonization destroys the information; this is a precise way to say that the loss ratio is intrinsically NOT an index of 'goodness' of X but a ratio of two prices, and must be characterized through its associated family rather than through a monotone hull.

(2010), 'Conic financial markets and corporate finance', Eurandom report (TU/e repository), and Madan & Schoutens (2016) 'Applied Conic Finance', CUP, Ch. 2-3 — definitions read in the TU/e copy only as 'cones defined by positive expectation under a concave distortion of the distribution function'; bid/ask formulas from memory.       STATEMENT: A market cone is A = {X : E_Q[X] ≥ 0 ∀ Q ∈ M} = {X : ∫ x dΨ^γ(F_X(x)) ≥ 0} with Ψ^γ a concave distortion (MINMAXVAR, MINVAR, …) at stress level γ ≥ 0. Bid price b_γ(X) = ∫ x dΨ^γ(F_X(x)) = inf_{Q∈M_γ} E_Q X (concave, monotone, cash-additive, positively homogeneous, law-invariant, comonotone-additive), ask price a_γ(X) = −b_γ(−X) = sup_{Q∈M_γ} E_Q X; b_γ ≤ E ≤ a_γ, with b_γ decreasing and a_γ increasing in γ. The acceptability level of a cash flow is γ*(X) = sup{γ : b_γ(X) ≥ 0}, an acceptability index by Cherny–Madan Thm 1 (M_γ increasing in γ). In the survival/loss convention (X ≥ 0 a loss), b_γ(X) = C_{g_γ}(X) with g_γ convex, a_γ(X) = C_{h_γ}(X) with h_γ = 1 − g_γ(1−·) concave. The ratio a_γ(X)/b_γ(X) ≥ 1 is scale invariant, law invariant and NOT monotone; conic finance uses the spread a − b, not the ratio, and proves no characterization of the ratio.
      USE: Identifies the 'conic picture' of F4 as a loss ratio: LR = C_h/C_g with h concave (ask/risk) and g convex (bid/premium) is literally ask/bid of a two-price market, LR ≥ 1, and it is quasi-convex (ratio of convex over concave positive functionals). Moreover the conic-finance acceptability index γ*(X) = sup{γ : b_γ(X) ≥ 0} is exactly of the Assa association type with a MONOTONE family, so the two pictures are related by: LR(X) ≤ β ⇔ a(X) − β b(X) ≤ 0, and the signed functional a − βb (F7) is monotone iff β ≤ 0 — again isolating F2 as the sign problem. The dual sets give LR(X) = sup_{Q∈M_h} E_Q X / inf_{Q'∈M_g} E_{Q'} X, a ratio of the ask and bid supports, which yields an immediate iff with hypotheses on the pair: LR is an ask/bid ratio of a two-price (conic) market iff ρ is coherent (sub-additive, comonotone-additive, law-invariant) and Π is the corresponding super-additive bid price.

(2009), 'Portfolio performance evaluation with generalized Sharpe ratios: beyond the mean and variance', Journal of Banking & Finance 33(7), 1242-1254 (SSRN 1028715) — from memory, citation located via OpenAlex; also Hodges (1998) 'A generalization of the Sharpe ratio and its applications to valuation bounds and risk measures', FORC preprint 98/88.       STATEMENT: For a utility U (increasing, concave, U(0)=0) and an excess return X, the generalized Sharpe ratio is GSR_U(X) := sqrt(−2 log(−U*(X))) where U*(X) := max_{a≥0} E[U(aX)] is the optimal expected utility of a leveraged position (Hodges' definition with U(x) = −e^{−x}: GSR = sqrt(−2 log(−max_a E[−e^{−aX}]))). Properties stated: (i) for X ~ N(μ,σ²) and exponential utility GSR_U(X) = μ/σ (the Sharpe ratio), (ii) GSR_U is scale invariant (depends on X only through the optimal leverage), (iii) GSR_U is monotone in X and consistent with second-order stochastic dominance when U is concave, (iv) Zakamouline–Koekebakker derive an adjusted-for-skewness Sharpe ratio ASSR = SR·sqrt(1 + (S/3)·SR) as a third-order expansion, with S the skewness, and show the ranking induced by GSR is utility-dependent. No iff characterization is proved; the only exact statement is the normal/exponential identity (i).
      USE: Shows the 'certainty-equivalent' route: a scale-invariant monotone index obtained as the optimal value of a leverage problem, which is a special case of the CM sup-formula with ρ_x(X) = −max_a E U(aX) − c(x). For the loss ratio this route is unavailable because LR is not an optimum of anything monotone in X; but the structural lesson is that the exponential/normal case is the only one where a ratio of two moments equals a utility-based index, i.e. ratio characterizations are typically about a SPECIFIC pair (mean, standard deviation) rather than an axiom set. This supports stating the loss-ratio theorem as an identification theorem for the pair (F3) with axioms on the associated family.
